  - **The seasonal inversion is the whole design, and the probe is what makes it a decision.** Hay is cut in **summer**, when the village has **0.7 spare hands**, and spent in **winter**, when it has **12.7**. A pasture is labour borrowed from the season with none and repaid in the season with nothing to do. That is why livestock is not merely "a berry patch that also works in winter", and it is the seasons spec's own rule — *a season with teeth is one the player prepares for* — arriving as arithmetic.
  - **The herd starves rather than merely stops growing** (Joe). A herd that cannot be lost is a free-money button and §2.3 gets nothing from it. The counterweight is written into the spec as a guard rather than a hope: a herd sized to its pasture with a barn built and hands available must survive an ordinary winter every time for 300 years, because **a pressure the player cannot answer is a leak, not a pressure** (D53's framing). And **the herdsman may not freeze** while the herd starves — §7 of the spec, which is what keeps the slice survivable without clothing.
  - **Hay gets its own barn and does not share the shed**, which is D52 refusing to be re-run: the shed is one room, packing it with logs left firewood nowhere to go and cost the village a third of its population for a century, and a third good in the same room adds a way to fail that the player cannot diagnose.
  - **Three slices** — pasture/herd/hay, then butchering, then clothing. The first ships with **no output at all**, bought entirely for the winter work and the pressure, which is the clearest statement yet that this is a labour system wearing a food system's clothes.
- **D59 · 2026-08-01 · Livestock is next, and herding is an *optional* food source rather than a tier every village climbs.** Joe, confirming the queue and correcting its framing in the same breath: *"herding is an optional food source, like fishing, orchards, etc. As the village scales, users can choose between different food sources."* That is a stronger statement than "livestock unblocks clothing", and it constrains the build in three ways:
  - **The derivation may never depend on it.** `RequiredGatherYield` solves for one weakest adult feeding themselves and three dependants **by foraging** (D16, D57), so meat has to be slack on top of that equation rather than a term in it. That is a property livestock happens to have for free — which is also the ordering argument for taking it before the 7-tile bound (D58), the one slice that *does* reopen the derivation chain.
  - **Optional means the sources have to genuinely differ, or the choice is a skin.** Herding's differentiators are structural rather than flavour: it **works in winter**, when foraging cannot; it **occupies land** — a pasture is an area the player paints, D42's brush shape, where every food source so far has been a generated point site; and it **yields something that is not food**, hides and wool, which is clothing's input.
  - **Winter demand is the thing to design against, and it does not arrive by itself.** D52 measured why winter is idle: the village already has its logs and `LabourQuota` genuinely wants nobody. A pasture that merely sits there over winter changes none of that, and clothing measures as a no-op a second time (`specs/clothing.md §5`). What creates the demand is **fodder** — animals eat when nothing grows, so somebody cuts hay in summer and carries it out to the herd in January, outdoors, every winter. That is D44's *"winter is a herding and slaughtering season"* with a mechanism under it instead of an intention.
  - **It puts clothing behind a single optional branch, and that is §2.7 working rather than a problem** — broad, not tall. The other road to a garment is cotton or flax from farming, which does not exist, so today there is exactly **one** road. Said out loud here rather than discovered later.
  - **Probed before building** (METHODOLOGY §3, the D53/D56 pattern), because if the labour system will not staff year-round outdoor work then herding's winter differentiator evaporates and it is just a berry patch that costs more. Two measurements, 300 years, shipped config and fixture, throwaway code since deleted:
    - **Winter is 86% idle, and that is the first time it has been a number.** A mean of **12.7 spare hands out of 14.7 able adults** through every winter, against **0.7 spare in summer** — and only 0.6% of winter ticks have nobody spare at all. The two configs agree to within 0.1. D52 said winter was "honestly empty" and left it there; this is the size of the hole livestock is being asked to fill, and it is enormous. **The binding constraint is the quota, not the labour** — livestock will be staffed if and only if its demand is written to ask, which is a design task rather than a risk.
    - **The cold model caps unclothed outdoor work at a third of winter, and can never kill.** `TrySeekWarmth` breaks a villager off at 3,000 of 6,000 and holds them at the fire until they are back to zero, so the steady cycle is *work 30−d, walk d, thaw 30+d, walk d* for a fire d travel-ticks away: **50% of winter at best, ~34% at the seven-tile bound.** Freezing outright needs 60 unbroken ticks outdoors, which under that bound is impossible by a factor of four. **So clothing will never save a life at the current bound — it buys roughly 3× the winter labour instead.** That is a better outcome than the one D45 imagined: it makes clothing an unlock rather than a tax, and satisfies *"clothing must never be mandatory"* by construction rather than by tuning. It also means the stakes escalate on their own once D58 lands and homes spread past 30 travel-ticks from a fire, at which point clothing quietly becomes life-or-death. `specs/clothing.md §5` is updated with both.
- **D58 · 2026-08-01 · The 7-tile bound comes off by per-site yield, not by work-in-place — and they are not two halves of one answer.** Joe's call, settling the disagreement §5 had flagged as blocking. Two docs described two mechanisms under one bullet, and choosing between them turned on a reframing neither had written down:
  - **The bound is not a rule villagers obey. It is a rule `Household.ChooseSite` obeys so that one global `gather_yield` can be solved.** The sim already walks honestly — a household further from work already makes fewer trips and already eats worse. Widening the bound does not break the simulation; it breaks the **guarantee**, which is that no family can be founded into starvation. That is a much smaller thing to give up than it looks, and it is why the smaller mechanism wins.
  - **Per-site yield makes distance *mean* something; work-in-place makes it *cheap*.** Amortise the commute over a multi-day stint and the whole map becomes workable — but distance stops costing, so the valley goes uniform and the pressure is deleted rather than made interesting. Per-site yield leaves the player a decision.
  - **It is two halves and both are budgeted: distance costs, *and* distant sites pay better.** Every forage site currently sits on a ring with the work in the middle, so per-site yield on its own is a tax on sprawl that buys nothing — inequality with no reason to accept it. The design is the frontier homestead beside a rich patch: isolated, and eating well for it. That is D32's interesting inequality.
  - **What is deliberately re-introduced is the failure mode `MaxHomeToWorkTiles` was created to fix** — `VillageEconomy.RoundTripTicks` records that budgeting for household #1 made every outlying family a rounding error that starved. Doing it on purpose is only allowed if the player can *see* it, so the hard bound becomes a score plus a warning, which is D43's "a site that is merely far is allowed and warned about" pattern already in the codebase.
  - **Work-in-place stays on the board, later, and after clothing.** It collides head-on with D53: a villager standing at a berry patch for ten days is outdoors for ten days against a fifteen-day threshold, which is either the best synergy available or a village that freezes in one winter. It also reopens the larder, the hearth, the birth gate and the market's §14.4 promise. It is the best of the options for §1.4 — *"he was away at the far wood the winter his daughter was born"* is a story the game cannot currently tell — and it would break D28's lockstep incidentally. None of that makes it the next thing.
- **D57 · 2026-08-01 · A housekeeping pass, and the rule that came out of it: a comment that describes deleted behaviour is a bug with a long fuse.** Joe called a pause before livestock — *"let's take a beat, tighten everything up to optimal efficiency and vision"*. Three read-only audits, then the repairs. What it turned up is worth more than the tidiness:
  - **Two live bugs, neither found by playing.** `Villager.DescribeState` covered ten of seventeen villager states and fell through to `State.ToString()`, so the panel whose whole job is explaining a villager printed `HaulingToStore` and `SeekingShelter` at the player — and all seven missing states were added *after* the method was written. And `CanBuildAt` asked an occupancy question that did not check homes, while `ChooseSite` asked its own copy that did, so a granary could be marked out on top of a family's house. **Both are the same shape: a rule written twice, one copy left behind.**
  - **The vision doc had the root number wrong.** D16 said the economy is derived for an adult feeding *two* children; `VillageEconomy.RequiredDependants` is **three**, and says in its own remark that two "produced a village sitting at break-even by construction". `CLAUDE.md` orders every session to read `DESIGN.md` first, so this was the single most expensive wrong sentence in the project.
  - **§6 said the phase headline was "not started" fifty lines above listing four of its slices ✅**, and "next up" pointed at a build menu that shipped on 28 July. `HANDOFF.md` carried a competing queue with **no items in common**, both labelled "Joe's call". §6 owns the queue now and the handoff points at it.
  - **Eleven dead symbols, a dead config key, and two tests asserting properties of a function nothing called.** Deleting `Household.PlacementFor` was the interesting one: the tests that used it were *checking where homes go*, against a square spiral that `ChooseSite` replaced three slices ago. They check real homes now.
  - **The XML docs compile.** `GenerateDocumentationFile` is on, which with `TreatWarningsAsErrors` makes a malformed comment a build failure. It immediately found two comments with two closing `</remarks>` each — half of both was being silently discarded — and two `cref`s pointing at members that no longer exist.
  - **The screenshot hook is permanent** (`BCLONE_SCREENSHOT`, `BCLONE_SCREENSHOT_YEARS`). D54 argued for this and D55 threw the code away again; the view has no tests at all (D11), so a repeatable way to *look* at it is the only verification it will ever have.
  - **The lesson, and it is the one to carry:** every stale comment found here was written as a forward note — *"until building placement exists"*, *"water is generated but nothing reads it yet"*, *"cold in ten days"*. They were all true when written and all became lies on a specific commit, silently. **A forward-looking comment is a promise to come back**, and this project had accumulated about twenty unpaid ones, several in `data/sim.config.json`, which is the modder-facing document.
- **D56 · 2026-08-01 · One name, one place — and a wood is not a thicket.** Joe read the bug off his own screen: *"Nobody is working the berry patch, the southern western thicket, the southern eastern thicket, the southern eastern thicket"*. A bearing has eight values and the village has six forage sites, so names collided — **44 valleys in 50 had at least one collision**, so this was the norm rather than an edge case. Every one of these names ends up inside a sentence about why somebody walks the way they do, and a name that points at two places answers nothing. That is §1.1, not tidiness.
  - **The worse half was the noun, and it is why this is a sim fix rather than a view one.** Every site past the first was called a *thicket* whatever it was, so a tree stand and a berry patch were named alike — a player told nobody was working "the southern eastern thicket" could not tell whether the village was short of **food or of timber**. Thickets are foraged; woods are felled.
  - **Collisions break by distance**, which is what a person reaches for: the near one keeps the plain name and the ones behind it become the far, farther and farthest. Ordered by distance then by tile, so it is a total order and two runs cannot disagree (D15). Bearings are hyphenated now — *"the northern eastern thicket"* was not a thing anybody says.
  - **Guarded rather than hoped for.** Fifty seeds, and no two places may share a name unless they are the same place — the market is deliberately a store *and* a workplace at one position (D36), so that pair sharing a name is the model being right. Plus the anti-vacuity half (D7): at least one valley must still need the tie-break, or the guard is watching a collision that never happens.
  - **The view's copy of the rule is deleted.** `Main.NameThem` collapsed duplicate names for a day; with the sim guaranteeing distinctness, a second copy is just a second place for the rule to be true, which is how two rules end up disagreeing.
- **D55 · 2026-08-01 · The valley fills the window and everything else floats on top of it — the Banished layout.** Joe's call, with a Banished screenshot as the reference and the brief *"it doesn't need to be perfect, I'm finding the info hard to read."* The old layout was one vertical stack — header, controls, map, panels underneath — so every panel competed with the map for height and the map got what was left. Now the map is full-window and behind everything, and five panels are pinned to the corners: what the village is doing top-left, what just happened top-right, whoever you clicked below that, who lives here bottom-left, the controls along the bottom.
  - **It deletes D54 rather than extending it.** D54 gave the alerts a fixed-height strip and an ellipsis because they shared a column with the map, so any change in their height moved the world. Joe's next screenshot showed what that bought: *"There is no one spa…"* instead of text running off the edge — tidier and no more readable. **A floating panel has neither problem**: the text wraps, the panel grows, and nothing else on screen notices. The fixed height, the padding to two lines and the ellipsis are all gone.
  - **The general lesson, and it is worth more than the layout:** the two symptoms Joe reported — the map jumping, and the sentence being cut off — were *the same bug*, and D54 treated them as two. They were both "the header and the map are in one stack". Fixing the stack fixed both and deleted the workarounds. **When two fixes fight each other, the thing they are both working around is the bug.**
  - **Godot detail worth keeping:** a control is clamped to its own minimum size by pushing its *right and bottom* edges outward, so a panel pinned to the bottom-right and asked to size itself grew off the screen and left one lit pixel in the corner. Panels have to be told to grow away from the edge they are pinned to. And a `Container` defaults to passing mouse events through — with a full-window map behind, every click on a panel would have placed a building underneath it.
  - Verified the same way as D54: screenshots from the viewport at 1640×1050 and 1280×800, checked by eye, with the throwaway hook removed before committing.
- **D54 · 2026-08-01 · Standing alerts get a fixed-height strip of their own, and no header line may ever wrap.** *(Superseded by D55 the same day — kept because its diagnosis of why the map moved is still the correct one, and because its fix being outlived by a day is the point D55 records.)* Joe watching the game: *"the viewing portal jumps around when the running log changes."* It did. Both standing alerts — nowhere to build (D42), work nobody is doing (D47) — were appended to the header label with a newline, so the header was one line tall, or two, or three, depending on what the village was short of. **The map is the only element with `ExpandFill`, so it absorbed the whole difference** — and `VillageMap.ToScreen` draws around the centre of its own rect, so a header growing by one line moved the entire valley on screen and changed how much of it fitted.
  - **The fix is to stop the layout reflowing at all, not to make it reflow gracefully.** The strip is always present and always exactly two lines tall — one per alert there can be — whether or not there is anything to say. Reserving the space costs a little of the window once; reflowing costs the player their place in the world every time a couple starts waiting for a house. Height is asked of the font, not written down.
  - **Nothing in the header wraps; it ellipsises.** Joe's screenshot lost *"There is no one spare to send."* off the right-hand edge. Wrapping is the obvious fix and it is refused, because **a label that wraps has a height that depends on the window width** — which puts the moving header straight back. An ellipsis is also the more honest failure: it says *there is more here*, where a hard clip just looks like the sentence stopped. The same treatment went on the placement message, which could push the staffing controls off the right of the window.
  - **The alert names at most three places and then counts.** Joe's line read *"…the southern eastern thicket, the southern eastern thicket"* — the same phrase twice, because **two different tree stands were generated with the same name.** The view now says distinct names only. **The collision itself is the sim's bug and is not fixed:** two workplaces a player cannot tell apart by name are two the game cannot explain (§1.1). Named here so it is not rediscovered by someone reading the view code.
  - **A hand-counted layout index went stale the moment the header changed**, which is this project's favourite bug in a new costume: `BuildBuildMenu` was told to insert itself at position `4`, so adding the alert strip silently moved the build menu *above* the time controls. It asks the controls node for its own index now.
  - **Verified by running it, because nothing here can be tested** (D11). Temporary instrumentation, since removed: a screenshot written from the viewport, and a print of the map's rect whenever it changed. Across 3,000 frames the alert text changed three times and **the map rect was logged exactly once, at frame 1.** That is the claim, measured rather than eyeballed — and the fact that it took throwaway code to make it is the argument for giving the view a permanent way to do this.
- **D53 · 2026-08-01 · Cold is positional and a fire *thaws* rather than resets — and the model fires when the woodpile fails, not on a timer.** D45 built, minus clothing; `specs/shelter-and-exposure.md`. `HearthSystem`'s burning is untouched and no number in the derivation chain moved. What changed is the chilling: `freezing_ticks` is deleted, `Villager.Cold` rises by 100 a tick on open ground and 60 under a fireless roof, and **any occupied home with firewood in it** gives 100 back — a neighbour's hearth counts, because the alternative makes you freeze in the correct doorway.
  - **One accumulator with three rates, not a counter per state.** The threshold is the *product* of the two tick-counts (6,000), which makes both rates exact integers for any day-counts a config can state — no common multiple, no rounding, no float (D2). A counter per row of D45's table leaves a hole in the ordinary case: a villager alternating between a fortnight outdoors and a spell in a cold room trips neither and is **immortal in conditions that should kill them**. There is a test for exactly that.
  - **The thaw is Joe's call (c), and it exists because the model was measured before it was built.** D45 as written *reset* the count at any fire. Probed over 120 years first: villagers spend **76% of winter standing at a lit hearth**, the longest unbroken spell outdoors anybody ever has is **under three days** against a threshold of fifteen, and the accumulator peaked at **60% of the way to freezing and never got closer.** Nobody would ever have frozen. Thawing keeps the sentence true — you never freeze at a burning fire — without letting one warm minute erase a fortnight in the snow. **A day by the fire undoes a day outdoors**, which is the whole derivation and needs no number of its own.
  - **And (d), stated forward: the woodpile is the thing that fails.** In a healthy village **cold still kills nobody** — 300 years, zero, peak 56%. That is the design, not a defect: the honest answer to *"why did somebody freeze?"* is now **the fuel chain failed**, and the guard is that breaking it in a way a player could cause does kill. Measured: a woodcutter who cannot keep up freezes the village. **Option (b) — moving the day-counts until a body count came out right — was refused**, because the day-counts describe a person in the cold and the death rate is a property of the economy. If cold is too rare, `WoodcuttersWanted` is the file to open.
  - **The epitaph is about the person now**, not their household's shelf: *out in the open*, or *under a roof with no fire under it*. It can also print *"beside a burning fire, which should not be possible"* — a bug said out loud rather than swallowed (METHODOLOGY §4), with a test that keeps it unprinted.
  - **A workplace has a roof; a berry patch and a tree stand do not.** That is what makes *outdoor work* a category the moment before clothing exists to fix it, and it is the asymmetry D19/D39's clothing tier will remove. Villagers break off work at **50% of the way to freezing** and walk to the nearest fire — a walk, not a teleport, so breaking off early is worth more than breaking off late.
  - **It costs almost nothing to run.** Asking every villager every winter tick what the tile under them is looked expensive — a first timing said the suite was a third slower — and re-measuring on a quiet machine put it at 4m55s against 4m47s. **The first number was contention, not the change**, which is the same lesson as D52's fetch counts one layer down: a measurement taken once is an anecdote. Kept as a reader rather than a cached position index (D47) — a cache here would be the bookkeeping-that-drifts shape this project keeps paying for.
  - **What the guards were nearly pointed at the wrong village.** The anti-vacuity test for *breaking off work to get warm* was first written against a settlement whose splitter yields one firewood, where it reads zero — not because nobody is cold but because everyone is dead by the first spring and there is no fire left to walk to. It is the **ordinary** village that proves it: somebody is walking in to get warm about 2% of every winter even when the woodpile holds. A guard that only fires in a corpse is not a guard.
- **D52 · 2026-07-31 · The idle winter is narrowed, not filled — and the "market regression" was never in the market.** Closes the WIP branch D44 opened. The half of D44 that was a real bug is kept: `LabourQuota.For` now asks what season it is, so the food floor is not staffed while there is nothing to pick, and the woodcutters and loggers the village genuinely wants get first call on every hand. The half that was invented is deleted: **sending every remaining winter hand to the woods**.
  - **What the fill actually did.** It was bounded by the tree stands' seats and by *"is any shed not yet full?"* — which is a bound on the shed, not on the work. Demand for timber is already answered twice further up the same method (`LoggersWanted` for houses, `LoggersToFeedTheHuts` for the fuel chain) and both are funded first, so **every hand the fill added was cutting logs the village had no use for, by construction.** Measured over 100 years with the market off: the sheds held **617–703 logs against a capacity of 717 for the whole run**. The shed is one room (D33), so firewood had nowhere to go — 60–285 standing anywhere in the village where it now runs 104–513 — and the birth gate reads a household's own firewood. **Mean population 14 where it now holds 22, with a full larder, a full shed, nobody starving and nobody freezing.** D33's recorded failure in mirror image; that one packed the shed with firewood and could not store logs.
  - **It presented as a market bug and cost a session.** `TheMarketShortensTheWalkForFood` failed, and the recorded hypothesis was that winter freed hands and over-staffed the market. It did not: `MarketersWanted` has been bounded by errands, never by spare hands, since D36. **The market arm was the healthy one** — marketers shuttled the scarce firewood out to the homes, so that village kept growing (mean 21) and only the *control* collapsed. Two consequences worth keeping: the market had quietly become **load-bearing**, which spec §14.4 promises it is not; and a test can fail because its control broke.
  - **The numbers, recorded here because this is the only place they survive.** Fetch-steps came to **17,995 with a market against 9,705 without** over 100 years, at mean populations of 20.6 and 14.2. The handoff that reported the regression had recorded **5,702 / 3,044** — which do not reproduce — with populations read at the end of the run rather than as a mean. Both wrong figures were quoted forward with confidence, which is why the rule is *a number in a handoff is hearsay until re-measured*.
  - **So the test measures a rate and checks its own control.** Fetch-steps are now **per person-tick**, because two runs of the same fixture do not hold the same number of people and whichever grows more walks more for the best of reasons. And the no-market arm must keep **at least three quarters** of the population the market arm holds, asserted *before* the comparison it is the control for — free, since both runs already happen. That quarter is the one chosen number rather than a derived one; the argument is §14.4's own, that the market buys convenience, and losing a quarter of the people is not an inconvenience.
  - **The winter that is left is honestly empty**, and that is the finding to act on rather than paper over: with the food floor gone the village still wants no extra hands, because it already has the logs. This is exactly D44's own forward note — **winter wants herding and slaughtering (D39's roadmap), not make-work in the woods.**
  - **Every wrong step here was reasoned from precedent and every right one came from a probe**, which is now the third session running that has been true.
- **D51 · 2026-07-31 · The player may say how many hands a workplace gets — never who.** Joe's call, and a **deliberate softening of §2.2** rather than an accident, so the line it still holds is written down. The pillar deletes *slotting a named worker into a building*; it does not delete the player having an opinion about staffing levels. An override sets a **count**, and proximity, household and catchment still choose the person — so every *"why is Elias at the stand?"* sentence stays true. **That is D42's zoning pattern applied to labour:** you paint the neighbourhood, the sim picks the tile.
  - **The default is load-bearing and was the one thing I pushed back on.** Joe's first shape was *default 1, else 0 with a warning*, which makes managing every workplace mandatory and turns a village of twenty buildings into a spreadsheet — non-negotiable 2. Agreed instead: **the default is null, "let the village decide"**, exactly today's derived quota, and an override is opt-in. A player who never touches one plays the village the quota describes; a player who wants to hand-staff the market can, and has the final say.
  - **Null and zero are different states**, and both are hashed. Null is *"I have no opinion"*; zero is *"nobody works here, I mean it"*. Conflating them would let a determinism test pass across a real divergence.
  - **D15's guard is narrowed, not deleted** (Joe confirmed). It used to scan for method *names* containing "Assign"/"Hire"; it now tests the **signature** — no public method may take both a `Villager` and a `Workplace`. That is the actual line, and it is stricter in the way that matters: `SetStaffing` would have sailed past the old name check anyway.
  - **`Workplace.Places` is the one place the override and physical capacity are reconciled**, so an override cannot be honoured by half the code and ignored by the other half — this project's most repeated bug.
  - **It was prompted by a market regression that turned out not to exist** (D52). The default never over-staffed the market — `MarketersWanted` counts errands, not spare hands — so the override stands on its own merits rather than as a fix for anything.
- **D50 · 2026-07-31 · Building capacities are derived too, and the shipped file had outgrown them.** Joe's call to take it as its own slice. Every re-derivation so far moved the *yields* — how much a trip brings back, how big a batch is — and left the buildings at capacities picked when a village was a dozen people. The shipped config had **three woodcutter seats where the economy required eight**, and three tree-stand seats where it required six. `VillageEconomy.RequiredWoodcutterSeats` already documents what that costs: a village that reaches the size the economy is budgeted for cannot physically make more firewood however many hands are free, and thirty-six people froze.
  - **It hid in the gap the handoff names.** `VillageFixtures.Village` derives its capacities, so every test was running a correctly-sized village while the game loaded an undersized one. The fix is a capacity assertion against the **shipped file**, which is the only place this could ever have been caught.
  - **Latent is not harmless, and the reason is new.** The village only holds about thirty people, so it never reached the twenty households the shortfall is measured against. But the player can now build granaries and grow past the old ceiling *on purpose* (D33, D43) — so the trap is sprung by succeeding. A pressure with no answer available is a known gap; a pressure you unlock by playing well is a bug.
- **D49 · 2026-07-31 · A season is thirty days, and pacing is stated as a life rather than a year.** Joe's call. Fifteen-day seasons made D45 unsayable — it states the danger of cold in *weeks*, and six weeks was longer than winter existed, so the model could not be expressed in the calendar the game kept. **The re-derivation is the interesting part, and it is not what you would guess:** `gather_yield` barely moves (47 → 46) because a longer year doubles both what an adult eats *and* the trips available to gather it — a trip is worth what a trip is worth, and lengthening the year does not change the physics of walking to a berry patch. `stockpile_target` **doubles** (46 → 95) because winter is the one place the cancellation fails: nothing can be gathered during any of it, and trips do not help inside a season with no food in it. Set to the *derived* values rather than to ones that merely pass — the guards only ask for "enough", and leaving 47 would have made food fractionally free for no stated reason.
  - **Pacing is now stated as *an average life takes 60 real minutes at 4×*** (Joe), which is the better statement: the generational loop is the core loop (§1.5), so how long a *life* takes to watch is the pacing decision and a year is merely its unit. `target_ticks_per_second` 1.0 → 1.5, playback only.
  - **⚠️ It only landed in half the project, and it took four commits to notice.** D49 changed `data/sim.config.json` and nothing else, so **every test went on running fifteen-day seasons and a 240-tick year while the game ran thirty and 480** — `VillageFixtures` inherits `DaysPerSeason` from `Phase0Fixtures`. Closed while starting D45, which is what exposed it: *"25 days sheltered without a fire"* only means anything inside a **30**-day winter, so against the fixture's fifteen it could never fire and every test for it would have been vacuous. **Cost of the fix: two assertions, both of which were wrong on their own terms** — one stepped a literal `240` and called it a year, the other demanded a granary ceiling double *exactly* when a floor division leaves a remainder. Phase 0's fixture keeps fifteen days; that is its own documented world. This is the third entry in a row (D48, D50, this) whose whole substance is the fixture-versus-shipped gap.
  - **It killed a village, and that turned out to be a discovery rather than a regression** — see D48. The change was exposing an existing leak, not causing one.
- **D48 · 2026-07-31 · Timber may never come to rest in a household's larder — and this is the third time.** `BehaviorSystem.UnloadAtHome` put whatever a villager was carrying into their household store, logs included, and a villager reaches home still holding timber whenever a trip is interrupted: the season turns, the yearly reshuffle takes their job, they stop to eat. **Nothing in the sim can spend a log in a larder** — the only code that reads `Household.Stockpile.Logs` is the state hash — so every one of those armfuls was dead for the rest of the run.
  - **Measured before fixing:** 240 logs frozen in two houses for twenty years, while a construction site wanted 28 more and a waiting couple wanted 30. Nobody starved and nobody froze; the village could not *build*, so no couple ever moved out, no new household formed, and it aged out with 5,300 food in the granary.
  - **The disguise is the point.** It does not present as a goods bug, it presents as a demographic wave — which is exactly how D34 hid for two phases. *A village that stops growing while its stores are full is a distribution bug until proven otherwise.*
  - **D25 is this bug with logs; D29 is it with firewood; D30 was supposed to close the class by moving goods into buildings, and did not.** So it gets an **invariant** rather than a fourth fix: a 300-year assertion that no household ever holds a log, run against **both** `VillageFixtures.Village` **and** the shipped config — they diverge, the leak was four times worse in the shipped one, and a guard watching only the fixture would have called it fixed. Plus an anti-vacuity test (D7) proving timber really is being moved during the window the invariant watches.
  - Timber now stays in the villager's arms and they turn round for a shed rather than teleporting, so goods still move only by trips people make.
- **D47 · 2026-07-31 · The village notices when a worker dies, and fills the gap if it can. ✅ Built.** Joe's call, and it is the answer to the cost D46 introduces. On a death: if there is an unassigned adult in the pool, they take the vacant job immediately rather than waiting for the next reshuffle; if there is nobody, **the UI says so and marks the building as unmanned or undermanned.** **Built as a reader, not a writer, and that is the part worth keeping:** both halves are computed from world state on demand — *"is anyone dead still holding a job?"* and *"which wanted work has nobody at it?"* — rather than maintained as flags. Nothing to hash, nothing that can be set and not cleared, and no determinism surface added. This project's recurring bug is code reading state from where it used to live, and a bookkeeping flag is exactly that shape. A death is answered one tick later, because `MortalitySystem` runs after `LabourSystem` and reordering them would be a behavioural change rather than a tidy-up (D5). This is what makes a three-year reshuffle affordable — the slow cadence stops mattering when the urgent case is handled the moment it happens. It is also §1.1 applied to labour: a workplace standing idle is currently silent, and silence is the one thing a legibility-first game cannot do.
- **D46 · 2026-07-31 · Labour reshuffles every three years, not every year.** Joe's call, revising D20's cadence rather than its reasoning. D20 already argued that a *seasonal* reshuffle churns jobs faster than the player can read the reason for holding one; a yearly one is the same objection at a lower volume. Three years is a rhythm a person can notice. **The cost is stated and answered separately:** a slower reshuffle means the village limps longer after a worker dies, which is exactly what D47 exists to fix. **`labour_reshuffle_years` reached the shipped config only with D52** — it was decided here and left at 1 in `data/sim.config.json` for four commits, which is the DESIGN-vs-config gap D50 is also a record of.
- **D45 · 2026-07-31 · Cold is about shelter and exposure, not household bookkeeping.** Joe's call, and it replaces the winter-severity mechanic drafted in `specs/environment-and-seasons.md §5.2`. The model:
  - **Temperature drops and being outdoors is dangerous.** A villager outside in winter with no clothing is in danger within about **two weeks**.
  - **Any residential shelter slows it drastically but does not stop it** — sheltered without a fire, a healthy adult is in danger within about **six weeks**.
  - ~~**Shelter with firewood actually burning resets the count to zero.** Not slows — resets.~~ **Superseded by D53:** a fire *thaws* rather than resetting. Measured before building it — villagers spend 76% of winter at a lit hearth, so a reset wiped the count constantly and killed nobody in 120 years.
  - **They break off and seek shelter at a stated threshold**, but still have to go out for food and work, which is what keeps it a trade rather than a hiding mechanic.
  - **Clothing — a manufactured good from leather, wool or cotton — removes the outdoor danger entirely.**
  - **Why it is better than what it replaces.** The shipped system is per-household accounting that produces a death; this one is *positional and watchable* — you can see somebody get cold, go inside, and warm up, which is §1.1 met by the picture rather than by the log. And it turns the §5.2 draft's problem inside out: severity-as-a-multiplier was a tax the player could not act on (the D37 objection to spoilage), whereas shelter and clothing are both things they build.
  - **The consequence that makes it structural: clothing is what unlocks winter as a working season.** Without it, sending a logger out in winter is genuinely dangerous; with it, it is not. That is §2.7's *unlock by doing* arriving out of a survival mechanic rather than a tech menu — and it gives the idle winter (D44) somewhere to go once the clothing chain exists. **Clothing therefore waits on D19/D39's production tier**, so every slice before it must be survivable without it.
  - **✅ Resolved (Joe, 2026-07-31), once D49 gave the calendar room for it.** Against 30-day seasons: **outdoors with no clothing, danger in 15 days — exactly half a winter. Sheltered with no fire, 25 days.** In ticks, 60 and 100.
    - **Note what the second number decides, because it is the one that was in doubt.** 25 days is *less* than a 30-day winter, so an unheated house **can** still kill you within one season — narrowly, and only if you are unheated for most of it. Had it landed above 30, `CauseOfDeath.Cold` would have gone dormant until clothing shipped, and D17's whole reversal with it. It did not, so fuel stays a live death axis the whole way through.
    - ~~The interim `freezing_ticks` of 80 (D49) is therefore close but not right: D45 moves it to 100 and splits it in two.~~ **Superseded by D53:** `freezing_ticks` was **deleted**, not moved. It is replaced by `exposure_days_outdoors` and `exposure_days_sheltered`, stated in days because they describe a person in the cold rather than a tick rate.
- **D44 · 2026-07-31 · Every forager sits idle all winter, and that — not the flat yield curve — is what winter is missing.** Found while checking D46: `BehaviorSystem` sends a forager home the moment the season turns, and `LabourAllocator` contains no reference to season at all. So a quarter of the village's working year is spent at home by everyone who holds the most common job, and no reshuffle ever notices. **This corrects an argument made the same day in `specs/environment-and-seasons.md`**, which claimed a seasonal forage yield curve would make winter a logging season. It would not — the mechanism is the labour pass, not the yield — and with a three-year reshuffle (D46) it certainly would not. The idle winter is a plain bug, it is true today at flat yields, it does not touch the derivation chain, and fixing it is worth more than the curve. **Taken first, on Joe's call.** — **Half reversed by D52:** removing the phantom forager floor was right; filling the gap by sending every spare hand to the woods was make-work that cost the village a third of its population. The forward note below is the part that survived.
  - **Joe's forward note: winter is eventually a herding and slaughtering season.** Cows, pigs, sheep, chickens and fish, plus the processed tier above them, give the village meat year-round once it has them — which is the honest answer to "what is winter work" rather than making everyone a logger. Extends D39's food roadmap and is recorded so the idle-winter fix is built as *"what else is there to do"* rather than as a special case for timber.
- **D43 · 2026-07-28 · Buildings are built, not bought; placement is free-form and warned rather than fenced; and only painted forest is ever felled.** Joe's answers to `specs/building-placement.md §11–12`, and three of them interlock into something none of them says alone.
  - **Construction costs labour and time**, not just materials: a site is marked, goods are hauled to it, and a **builder** raises it — competing for hands in the same quota as everything else, and yielding first, because §4a's standing policy is that *a village short of hands feeds itself before it builds*. Same argument as D14 and D29: things that happen in this village are work somebody does.
  - **Placement is free-form, with warnings rather than fences.** Hard refusals stay hard — water, occupied ground, off the map — because those are impossibilities, not judgement calls. Everything else is the player's call, told plainly at the time. **Demolition returns some materials and may strand a store's contents**, which is the same lesson D34 taught about a dead family's larder. (*"Move" is assumed to mean demolish-then-place, which needs no new machinery; flagged in the spec for one word of confirmation.*)
  - **Unpainted forest is never cut; trees regrow slowly on their own; planting is gated behind managed forestry.** Taken together these form a loop, and **removing any one breaks it**: you may only fell what you paint, so felling is always a decision; a valley cleared too eagerly is a mistake you can see; **natural regrowth is what makes that mistake survivable rather than terminal**; and deliberate planting, once earned, makes it recoverable on purpose instead of by waiting. Planting-only regrowth would lock a player out of the fix at exactly the moment they needed it, since the unlock takes generations — **natural regrowth is what keeps the gate fair.**
  - **Two consequences that need handling rather than noting.** A founding village with nothing painted fells no logs, makes no firewood and freezes in its first winter — so **the exiles arrive with a starter harvest zone and residential zone already painted**, which doubles as a gentle tutorial. And **planting cannot ship until §2.7 exists**, which splits forestry: the harvest brush now, the planting brush as the tech tree's first node. That ordering is right rather than merely necessary — the harvest brush is what *creates the pressure* managed forestry answers, and a node that resolves a problem the player has actually felt is worth far more than one that arrives first.
  - **Derived, not picked (D16):** the regrowth rate is stated as *a valley cleared by a village of thirty takes about a generation to come back on its own* — which makes deforestation a **generational** mistake, §1.5's core loop, and gives managed forestry something concrete to beat.
- **D42 · 2026-07-28 · The player paints zones of intent; the village acts on them when it has a reason to.** Joe's call, following *Foundation*. **Homes are not placed one at a time** — the player brushes a **residential area**, and villagers build individual homes inside it, positioned by the sim, *only when a home is actually needed*. No need, no houses. When the village outgrows the painted area the player is **prompted** to paint another. Stores and workplaces are still placed individually; the brush is for things the village produces many of, over time.
  - **It resolves the tension `specs/building-placement.md §3` was built around, rather than trading it off.** Handing per-house placement to the player would have broken `MaxHomeToWorkTiles` — a bound the *sim* keeps and the food economy is derived against. Zoning keeps it: `Household.ChooseSite` is unchanged, just constrained to the zone. And the warning about a bad neighbourhood moves from a nag on every house to **one check when the area is painted**, which is both kinder and more likely to be read.
  - **It is §2.2's philosophy applied to building.** The player sets conditions and agents respond, exactly as workplaces state a catchment and villagers take jobs by proximity. You shape the field; you do not command anybody. It is also §1.2 satisfied by design rather than by restraint — paint once, get twenty houses across fifty years.
  - **The same brush extends to forestry, and that is the bigger idea: which forest to CUT, and where to PLANT.** It turns `JobKind.Logger` from a worker at an inexhaustible "tree stand" into someone felling the trees you designated — so **forest becomes a resource with a place and a quantity**, deforestation becomes visible on the map, and §2.3's *"resource radii exhausting and forcing expansion"* finally has something behind it. Planting is the answer to it, and it is **already promised as §2.7's example of unlock-by-doing**: *"log a forest for two generations → managed forestry."* The tech tree gets its first concrete node, arriving from the direction §2.7 says it should — out of how the town has lived.
  - **Cost to respect, and D41 called it in advance: this makes terrain mutable.** The travel-cost flow fields are cached with no invalidation path precisely because `GeneratedMap` never changes. Felling happens not to affect walkability, so it survives by luck — planting and bridges will not. **The invalidation path must be built when terrain first changes**, not the first time somebody notices a stale route. Zones are player intent and therefore sim state: saved, hashed, part of the determinism contract.
  - **Sequencing: the residential brush first** (it changes no terrain and placement is not playable without it); **the forestry pair as its own slice** (mutable terrain, cache invalidation, a tree-growth rate derived rather than picked, and it reaches into two pillars). Landing them together would be two hard things at once, which this project has now learned about twice.
- **D41 · 2026-07-28 · Travel cost is a flow field per building, not a search per call — and "dry" was never the same as "reachable".** Implementing D40 needed real pathfinding in the one shared cost field, which is read by labour catchment, market errands, household placement and the economy's budget: the busiest question in the sim, asked N villagers × M workplaces every labour pass. A path search per call would have been the obvious implementation and the wrong one.
  - **The property that made it cheap: every travel query in this game has a building at one end.** Checked against every call site — home→workplace, position→store, position→home, candidate-tile→site. Nothing ever asks the distance between two arbitrary tiles. So one Dijkstra flow field per building answers *both* questions an agent has — how far, and which way to step — as array lookups, with no stored routes and nothing to invalidate. ~40 fields over a 9,600-tile valley is about 1.5 MB, built when a building is founded rather than in the tick loop.
  - **Cached with no invalidation protocol, deliberately**, because `GeneratedMap` is immutable: a field computed for a destination is correct for the whole run. That removes the likeliest home for the "code reading state from where it used to live" bug this project keeps meeting. **When terrain becomes mutable — a felled stand, a paved road, a bridge — that stops being true and the cache needs a way to be dropped.**
  - **Ties are broken by tile order, not by a priority queue.** A binary heap is asymptotically better and would have broken determinism invisibly: equal-cost tiles pop in an order that depends on the heap's internal shuffling, so two runs of one seed produce different — equally short — routes and the state hash diverges on a journey nobody chose differently.
  - **The bug this flushed out, and it is the same shape as always.** Buildings were placed at fixed offsets from the founding site with no terrain check, so on seed 1 the shed and the woodcutter's hut both came down *in the river*: no logs could be stored, no firewood made, and all four founders froze in the first winter. Nothing in the log said "your shed is in the water" — it said they were cold. **Being dry is not enough either**; a building on the far bank is exactly as useless and looks fine on the map. Placement now asks *reachability from the founding site*, and the founding site itself is chosen as the land mass holding the most work.
- **D40 · 2026-07-27 · Water is impassable until the village learns to bridge it — and a bridge is a technology, then a building.** Joe's call, and it is the answer to `specs/seeded-map-generation.md §10.1`. **Wood bridges first, stone upgrades later.** This is the single best fit for the design the project has found so far, because it lands on four pillars at once: it is §2.5's terrain *dictating viability* rather than decorating it; it is §2.7's tech tree attaching to something you can point at on the map; it is §2.6's desire paths given a genuine funnel, since every crossing on that side of the river runs through one tile; and it is a placement decision (D38) whose value the player can see before they commit. A river you can stroll across is scenery. A river you must go *round* until you can afford not to is the map arguing with you.
  - **Engineering consequence, and it is not small: this needs real pathfinding.** *(Written before D41 built it; at the time)* `TravelCostField.Cost` was Manhattan distance and `GridPos.StepToward` walked in a straight line — neither knew terrain existed. Impassable water means a genuine path search over the grid, and that field is read by labour catchment, market errands, and the economy's distance budget, i.e. by the things that decide who eats. It is also the field §2.6 will layer trample costs onto. **This is its own slice and must not be smuggled into worldgen**, or two hard things fail together and neither can be diagnosed. Sequenced explicitly in the map spec.
  - **Corollary the generator inherits:** until bridges exist, the generator must guarantee the founding village is not cut off from the work it needs. That is a real constraint on generation, not a hope.
- **D39 · 2026-07-27 · An infinite winter buffer is allowed — it just has to be *earned*. This corrects D37's reasoning.** Joe's call. D37 argued that storage capacity answers what spoilage was for, because a bounded granary means no unlimited bank. **That was wrong about the design.** The player can build **several granaries**, and can unlock a larger granary through the tech tree, so the buffer is not capped at all — it is *priced*. A village that has put the resources and the labour into ten granaries has genuinely solved winter, and it should feel like it: that is an achievement the player can point at, not an exploit. **The counterweight to a bank is its cost, not its impossibility** — which is a better answer than either spoilage or a hard cap, and it is the one consistent with §1.1, because the player can trace exactly what they spent to get there.
  - **So D37's conclusion stands and its argument does not.** Spoilage is still cut, for the reasons D37 gives about fun and babysitting. But granary capacity is *not* the thing holding winter at bay, and it must not be treated as load-bearing in that way — the earlier note saying so is superseded.
  - **Joe's named food roadmap, recorded so D19 stops being vague:** farming crops, herdsmen and butchering, gathering, fishing — and then the processing tier that makes them interesting: **baking bread from wheat, wine from berries, beer from wheat.** That is several processing chains of the shape D29 built once, which is exactly what §2.2 says processing is for, and it is where §2.7's "unlock by doing" will live.
- **D38 · 2026-07-27 · Most buildings are multi-instance; a few are singletons.** Joe's call. The player builds as many granaries, sheds, markets, huts and homes as they can afford; a small number of civic buildings — a town hall is the example — exist once. Recorded because **the code currently assumes the opposite**: `SimWorld.Granary`, `.StorageShed` and `.Market` each return *the first* store of their kind, and `VillageEconomy.PopulationCeiling` derives from a single granary's capacity. Those are fine as long as there is one of each and become silently wrong the moment there are two, which is a placement-shaped bug waiting to happen. The fix belongs with placement, and this entry exists so it is found before it bites rather than after. — **✅ Closed 2026-07-28** by placement slice 1: those three members are gone, and the compiler enumerated all fifteen call sites.
- **D37 · 2026-07-27 · Food does not spoil. Cut from the plan — and the problem it was for is already solved.** Joe's call: *"it's not fun."* He is right, and it is worth saying why rather than just deleting the line. Spoilage is a tax that arrives as a number going down for no reason the player took a decision about — it punishes the well-run town exactly as hard as the badly-run one, it makes the granary feel like a leaking bucket rather than an achievement, and it adds a chore (watch the store, cycle the stock) to a game whose second non-negotiable is *reduce babysitting*. It fails §1.2 and §1.1 at once.
  - **The reason it was on the list is real, and it now has a better answer.** D32 named the danger precisely: a granary that keeps food perfectly forever is a bank, and a village with a bank has permanently solved winter, which undoes much of §2.5. But **storage capacity (D33) bounds the granary**, so there is no unlimited bank to have — the village cannot stockpile its way out of winter permanently, because the building will not hold it. The pressure survives; it just comes from *how much you have built* rather than from food quietly rotting. That is strictly the more legible of the two, and it is the one the player can act on.
  - ~~**Worth keeping in mind:** this makes granary capacity load-bearing in a second way. It is now the only thing standing between the village and an infinite winter buffer, so it should not be quietly relaxed to fix some unrelated squeeze.~~ **Superseded the same day by D39 — this was wrong.** The player can build several granaries and unlock bigger ones, so the buffer is priced rather than capped. The conclusion (cut spoilage) stands; this particular argument for it does not.
- **D36 · 2026-07-27 · The market is manned, it delivers, and it is the only thing that can reach a dead family's larder.** Joe's call, and it closes D30 and delivers the building half of D14 — *"distribution is a job, not a slider"* — after three phases of that being a promise. `JobKind.Marketer` is **the first job that produces nothing**: a forager makes food appear and a woodcutter turns one good into another, but a marketer only ever moves what already exists. Several traders work it, per Joe.
  - **"If the distances make sense" needed no threshold.** A marketer **never walks empty-handed**, and every leg is chosen cost-first from wherever they are standing right now — the same principle D15 and D23 already use, off the same travel-cost field. So *"pick up food from the granary on the way back"* is not a special case: after a delivery near the granary, the granary is simply the cheapest next stop. There is no detour to evaluate because there is no round trip, only the next-cheapest useful leg.
  - **It is deliberately the lowest-priority job in the village, and yields first.** Fetching is untouched (spec §3): households still walk to a store for themselves, so an unstaffed market means longer walks and stranded goods, **never a household that cannot eat**. That is asserted by a test that runs the whole 300 years with the market switched off. A distribution system that becomes load-bearing is the cliff the founding village falls off.
  - **Measured: goods stranded in empty houses fall by 98%** (1,618 goods-years against 81,846 with no market), which is the loose end D34 left. Fetch trips shorten by only 6% — honestly modest, because the shipped market stands a couple of tiles from the granary. **That is a placement question, not a market question**, and it is precisely what makes building placement the moment this system starts paying the player back.
  - **The expensive mistake, recorded:** collecting *surplus* from living households — the obvious generalisation — killed the village at five people. A home is above target every time its forager walks in the door, so marketers stripped families the moment they got ahead and the families fetched it straight back; the granary stopped filling and the birth gate never opened. **A trader moves what nobody is using, not what somebody has just earned.**
  - **Known seam:** the market is both a `StoreBuilding` and a `Workplace` at one position, which are two types today. Merging them into the single `Building` the spec's §4 describes is the right end state and was not worth doing inside this slice.
- **D35 · 2026-07-27 · The dead will eventually be buried in a cemetery; until then they simply go.** Joe's call, prompted by D34 — the bug where the dead never left their household's member list was, read diegetically, a village full of unburied corpses. **For now a death removes the person from the village's living concerns and nothing marks the spot**; the life log and the epitaph remain the only trace, which is where a Phase 0 villager's death already lives (D8). **A cemetery is the intended form**, and it is a better fit for this game than it looks at first: a graveyard is the town's memory made visible, the same way a paved road is a fossilised decision (§2.6) and a known technique is a fragile inheritance (§2.7). It is also somewhere a name can persist after the person, which is the difference between "34 people died" and a village that remembers Mabel. Not designed yet, and deliberately not built alongside storage — it wants the same treatment as a real building (a place, a catchment, work someone does), which means it belongs with placement rather than before it.
- **D34 · 2026-07-27 · The dead were taking up room in the house, and that is what had been killing every village.** A household's member list keeps everyone who ever lived there — `RemoveMember` is called when somebody *moves out* and never when somebody *dies* — and the birth check read that list's length as "how many live here". So a household that had seen `max_household_size` people pass through it was permanently barred from having another child, with a young couple in it and every other condition met, even once all seven were in the ground. **Households ratcheted one way into sterility, so every settlement died out about a century in regardless of its food, its fuel, or its storage.** Found by running 300 years instead of 150 and asking, per household per year, which condition was refusing a birth. It hid for two phases because it looks exactly like the population wave it coincides with — a slow demographic decline is what you would *expect* to see. Every other occupancy question in the sim already asked `LivingMembersOf`; this was the one place that did not.
  - **The test window was the reason it survived.** The acceptance run watched 150 years, and the collapse completed at about year 180: the village was at twenty-three and falling when the test stopped looking, which read as the tail of a wave rather than the middle of an extinction. 150 had never been chosen against anything. **The acceptance run is now 300 years and asserts the village is still there at the end**, plus that it held a *band* rather than merely surviving — because "never dropped below the founding four" was satisfied for a century and a half by a village in the process of dying.
  - **Corollary worth keeping: an assertion about a window is not an assertion about a system.** Every long-run test here should be asked what it would do if the run continued.
- **D33 · 2026-07-27 · Storage capacity is what decides how big the village gets, and one stated number sets it.** Slice 5 of `specs/storage-and-distribution.md`, taken ahead of the market on Joe's call. A granary holds a winter's store for `granary_feeds_people`; `VillageEconomy` derives the quantity from that and the **population ceiling** from the quantity, so the ceiling is a consequence rather than a setting (D16). Births are already gated on the granary holding a share of what everyone alive would want — a demand that grows with the village and used to be unbounded — so capping the building caps the settlement. **Measured over 200 years: the village holds 24–35 people, against 24–86 with the cap removed.** That is the flat line Joe asked for, and the mechanism is the one he predicted: growth stops at what the buildings support instead of overshooting them and falling back.
  - **How big a granary is counts as content, not economy** — a fact about the building, like how many hands fit at a berry patch — so it lives in the config where a modder can change it. When placement lands, this is the moment storage starts paying the player back: the answer to "we have outgrown the granary" becomes *build another one*, and **where** it goes is the first decision in the game that storage makes interesting. Until then the pressure is real and the response to it is a config line, which is the same gap `RequiredWoodcutterSeats` already carries.
  - **Capacity is total across goods, not per good.** A shed packed with logs has nowhere to stack firewood, and being made to choose is the point; three independent shelves that never compete would be bookkeeping wearing a constraint's clothes. Measured in both directions — an over-eager fuel quota packed the shed with six hundred firewood, logs could not be deposited, no house was ever raised again, and the village dwindled to three people without a soul freezing.
  - **Two bugs it flushed out, both the same shape as the ones already logged.** `TargetFoodForTheGranary` was answering two questions — *can we feed another mouth?* (must stay unbounded; that IS the ceiling) and *should anyone go out and work?* (must be bounded by what fits, or the answer is yes forever) — which is D21 again. And `WoodcuttersWanted` counted firewood stacked in *other people's homes*, which no household can fetch, so a surplus in one house cancelled a shortage in another; the justification in the comment was a sharing policy that slice 3 had already deleted.
- **D32 · 2026-07-27 · Food gets its own building — a granary — separate from the shed that holds materials.** Joe's call, and it settles the question D30 was going to have to face: whether one undifferentiated store would quietly delete the inequality D14 exists to create. It would have — *"one family starving beside a thriving neighbour"* is the story per-household food was introduced for, and a single village-wide pile makes it unexpressible. Two buildings keep it, and **change what inequality is made of, for the better**: it stops being about whose larder it is, which is an accident of which house a forager was born in, and becomes about **distance and hands** — a household far from the granary, or with nobody spare to send, eats worse than its neighbours. That is spatial, watchable, and it ties straight into catchment (§2.2) and desire paths (§2.6) instead of sitting off to one side. A story about a family on the wrong end of the valley beats one about a family with the wrong surname. It is also just the honest division: food spoils and timber does not, which is why no village has ever kept them in the same shed.
  - **Named for later, not now:** if granary food never spoils, the village has a bank, and a village with a bank has permanently solved winter — which undoes much of §2.5. Spoilage is the counterweight and belongs with Phase 2's environment work. Recorded because it changes what the food economy is derived against, and that derivation should not be redone twice.
  - **Superseded 2026-07-27 — see D37. Spoilage is cut, and capacity turned out to answer the same question better.**
- **D31 · 2026-07-26 · The village should survive at a *stable size*, not by growing.** Joe's call, and it settles what the acceptance tests are actually for. Failure has to remain possible — §2.3 is built on pressure and §1.1 promises the player understands the town's death — so a test asserting "the population grows" was quietly asserting the wrong thing. What the baseline must show is that an *unpressured* village holds steady; a village that dies should die because of something, and right now there is no player to blame, so a dying baseline can only mean the constants are wrong. **The corollary worth remembering when the player gains real choices: at that point a collapse becomes a legitimate outcome, and these tests should be re-read rather than defended.**
- **D30 · 2026-07-26 · Goods live in buildings, not only in households.** Joe's call, and it supersedes the per-household stores for *materials*. A small amount of firewood sits at the woodcutter's workshop; the rest goes to a **general-purpose storage shed** (stone, logs, lumber, cloth, firewood), to the **marketplace**, and to villagers' homes. Three things follow. It is the concrete form of D14's "distribution is a building, not a policy slider" — the seasonal food and daily firewood sharing policies are placeholders that storage plus a market should delete together. It gives §2.6's desire paths something real to be about, because hauling between buildings is *traffic*. And it fixes by design a bug that has now been found twice: goods piling up where they cannot be spent (logs in the logger's house, D25; firewood in the woodcutter's house, D29) — a shed is the answer both times. **Not built yet**; it needs its own spec, and it should probably land alongside D19's extra workplaces since both are about buildings having jobs and contents.
  - **Status, appended 2026-07-27:** built, slices 1–3 of `specs/storage-and-distribution.md`. All four workarounds named above are deleted. It did *not* land alongside D19 — the extra workplaces are still pending, and that turned out not to matter. The market (slice 4) and capacity (slice 5) remain.
- **D29 · 2026-07-26 · Wood is a two-stage chain: a logger fells **logs**, a woodcutter turns logs into **firewood**, and firewood is the fuel.** Joe's call, following *Banished*. Logs build; firewood burns. **This is the project's first secondary processing chain, and that is the significant part** — §2.2 already calls processing structural rather than content ("processing is where the tech tree attaches to daily life"), and every workplace so far has been a pure producer. A workplace that *consumes an input to make an output* can be idle for want of logs rather than for want of a worker, which needs a new refusal reason and a **two-stage quota** where demand propagates back down the chain. Doing that on something as simple as firewood is the point: every processing chain after this one inherits the shape. Note the naming cuts against everyday usage — colloquially a woodcutter fells trees — so the tree-stand job is renamed `Logger`.
  - **Firewood is shared between households**, on the same cadence and the same keep-your-own-floor rule as food. Cold is deliberately *parallel* to hunger rather than harsher than it, so the player keeps one mental model instead of two.
  - **Forward-looking, recorded so it is not lost:** firewood is eventually **traded at the trading post** (§2.4 — trade has not been designed yet) and **distributed by the market building** (D14). Both are reasons to keep firewood a first-class resource rather than a counter on a household, and the seasonal sharing policy shipped with it is the *same placeholder* the food sharing is — the market should delete both together.
  - **Tools wait for a workshop to be made at** (Joe's call). A tool materialising from a household's woodpile by policy is the same abstraction as the food-stall slider D14 replaces and the worker-slot §2.2 deletes. When they land they hang off the villager, not the household, so §2.1 can attach skill without a migration.
- **D28 · 2026-07-26 · Time-on-task becomes personal — but in Phase 4, with the skill pillar.** Joe's call: vigour, and later skill (§2.1), should change *how long* a job takes and not only how much it yields. That is the fix for villagers moving in lockstep (measured at 99.9% — see §5), and it is the most diegetic of the options because it makes ageing something you watch rather than read: an old villager would not just bring back less, they would be *out longer*, deepening D12. **Deferred deliberately**, for two reasons. It is a §2.1 change and METHODOLOGY §1 forbids building pillars in parallel; and it forces `VillageEconomy` to re-derive trips-per-year from a worst-case round trip, which would reopen a food economy that has only just been stabilised — on a phase that has just passed its Success Test. The cheap stopgap, if the duos start to grate first, is a seeded per-villager rhythm drawn once at birth; that treats the symptom, and it can be pulled forward on its own.
- **D27 · 2026-07-26 · No camera rotation until the view has depth.** Joe asked for middle-mouse rotate alongside pan and zoom, and it was deferred once it was clear what it would actually do: the view is flat top-down, so rotating it spins the map like paper on a table rather than orbiting it the way *Banished* does. That is a different feature, and building it now would mean building it twice. Middle mouse is deliberately left unbound so the binding is free when the view gains an angle.
- **D26 · 2026-07-26 · The world has bounds — a 120×80 valley — and the map has a camera.** The view used to auto-fit every workplace each frame, which was survivable with one berry patch and became useless with seven: the settlement rendered as a smudge three tiles across in the middle of an empty panel, and the villagers, all standing on the same few tiles, drew as one dot per house. So framing became something you do rather than something that happens: WASD pans, the wheel zooms about the cursor, and both stop at the valley's edge. **Bounds live in `SimConfig`, not in the view**, because seeded map generation (D18) will generate terrain into exactly this rectangle — at which point it stops being a drawing hint and becomes world state. Wide rather than square, for the river valley in §2.5.
  - **Corollary: people on the same tile are fanned apart on a small ring**, by rank within the tile, so four adults resting at one house read as four people. View-only; sim positions never move. This is the phase's Success Test in miniature — "watching twelve villagers is still legible" cannot be answered if twelve villagers draw as three dots.
  - **Corollary: one control decides how much explanation is on screen** — off, the selected villager, or everyone — governing both the home-to-work route lines and the catchment rings. The route line is the visual counterpart of `JobReason`: the sentence says *"the tree stand was nearer at 2 tiles, but the village has all the woodcutters it needs"*, and the line shows the same thing without a click. Seven catchment rings drawn permanently were most of the clutter and none of the meaning.
- **D25 · 2026-07-26 · A new house is paid for by the whole village, the two parent households first.** Drawing only from the parents looked right — families provide for their children — but timber is cut by whoever lives nearest the stand, who is very often nobody's parent. So the village cut wood year after year, piled it in the woodcutter's own house where it could not be spent, and no home was ever built: every settlement stalled at a handful of houses and aged out *without a single villager starving*. Raising a house is communal work, and the store it comes out of is the village's. (This is not a retreat from D14 — food stays per household, because starving beside a full neighbour is the story D14 exists to make possible. Nobody starves for want of timber.)
- **D24 · 2026-07-26 · Forage sites are spread the way the homes are, and catchment drops to 10 tiles.** D19 said "more than one food source"; that turned out not to be sufficient on its own. Putting all the extra sites out at the map edges left every home near the *middle* of the village competing for the one original berry patch, so tightening catchment did not restrict outlying households — it left central ones idle beside a full patch, and they starved. A ring of sites at roughly the width of the settlement, plus two further out, is what lets catchment bind survivably. Ten tiles is the shipped radius: at twelve a central home could reach nearly everything, so the rule constrained nothing.
- **D23 · 2026-07-26 · The work the village needs least of is allocated first.** A single cost-sorted pass over every workplace — the spec's design — lets everyone near the tree stand take an even nearer berry patch first, so the one timber job falls to whoever is left over, who is by construction the most remote person in the village and often cannot reach the stand at all. The job went unfilled and the village stopped building. Filling scarce work first hands it to whoever genuinely lives nearest, costs food nothing (the quota has already decided what can be spared), and is the more explainable of the two: "Elias cuts timber because he lives nearest the stand" beats "because he was the last one left".
- **D22 · 2026-07-26 · Timber demand is derived from what wood is for, plus a standing woodpile.** Sparing every hand food did not need put *half* a founding village on the tree stand — one cutter yields enough for several houses a year, and wood is simply much cheaper than food. So the timber quota is asked the same question as the forager quota: how much does the village need? Answer: the homes couples are waiting for, **plus one more kept in store**. That last clause is not padding — cutting only what is currently needed turns timber from a job into an errand (a hand goes to the stand at the new year, cuts thirty logs by midspring, and is taken off again), and it halved the village's growth rate. A village that has been through a winter keeps a woodpile.
- **D21 · 2026-07-26 · Labour demand is two questions, not one: local capacity and a village quota.** `Workplace.LabourDemand` became `Workplace.Capacity` — how many hands physically fit — and the village-level question ("how many foragers does this settlement need?") moved to `LabourQuota`, where it can actually be answered. Four different values of the old single field each broke the village in a different way (`specs/labour-allocation.md §3`); none could have worked, because no local field can express a global constraint. The forager quota is a **floor**, not a ceiling: a ceiling leaves able adults idle, and with food stored per household (D14) an idle adult is not a spare resource, they are a household producing nothing.
- **D20 · 2026-07-26 · Labour reshuffles periodically rather than being pinned by rules.** Joe's call, following *Banished*: the village re-runs its labour allocation on a cadence, so workers drift toward jobs near where they live. Chosen over a hard "one forager per household" floor because a reshuffle is a *behaviour the player can watch*, whereas a floor is a rule they must be told; and because it self-corrects the cases a floor cannot — a forager dying, or a family moving house. Implication: the allocator must be re-runnable from scratch rather than incremental. Constraint: a job change must state its own reason, or the player sees people inexplicably swapping jobs.
- **D19 · 2026-07-26 · Food will come from several kinds of workplace, plus secondary processing.** Fishing, gathering and hunting huts as raw sources; smokehouse/mill-style processing above them. Not a content wishlist: the measured finding is that a *binding* catchment radius kills outlying households when there is only one food source, so multiple sources are the prerequisite for §2.2's central rule working at all. Processing is also where §2.7's "unlock by doing" gets somewhere to live. First step is just more than one raw source.
- **D18 · 2026-07-26 · The map will be generated from the run's seed, and it is the same seed as the sim.** Terrain, water, forest stands, and forage sites get generated rather than hand-placed, so a second playthrough is a different *place*. Because the sim is already deterministic and seeded, map generation must draw from the same seeded RNG in a fixed order — then a single number reproduces an entire run including its world, which is what makes bug reports and shared seeds work. Positions currently live in config as literal coordinates; those become generator output. Not built yet.
- **D17 · 2026-07-26 · Wood serves all three purposes: winter fuel, building material, and tools.** Joe's call. Fuel makes winter bite on a second axis; building material gates new houses, which ties household formation to labour so the village can only spread as fast as it can build; tools raise gather yield and feed the skill pillar (§2.1) later. Three consumers is what makes "forage or cut timber?" a genuinely contested decision rather than a cosmetic one — a village that only forages can never grow.
  - **Clarified (Joe, 2026-07-26): "building material" means every building, not just homes**, and tools are in rather than possible. That matters to `LabourQuota.WoodcuttersWanted`, which derives timber demand from what the wood is *for* — currently one term (homes for couples waiting) plus a standing woodpile. Each new consumer is another term in the same sum, and together they are what turns cutting from an occasional errand into a livelihood somebody holds. **Measured symptom to fix, not a prediction:** with homes as the only consumer, a village with a full woodpile correctly staffs nobody at the stand, so §2.2's "trees do not stop in winter" advantage currently has almost nothing to bite on (see `specs/labour-allocation.md §4a`).
  - **Tension to respect:** the `+1` woodpile reserve in the timber quota is a stand-in for continuous demand. When the other consumers land, check whether it is still earning its place or has become double-counting.
  - **Tension to respect:** Phase 0 explicitly rejected warmth as "a second overlapping death system" (`specs/phase-0-vertical-slice.md §3`). Reintroducing fuel-as-survival is a deliberate reversal, defensible now that there are households to insulate and a labour system to trade off against — but the Phase 0 reasoning still applies to *legibility*. A death must never be ambiguous between cold and hunger; the log has to name which one killed someone.
